a:T605,This is a product announcement rather than a technical paper, so the training recipe is not disclosed and should not be over-inferred. Still, several technical directions are visible from the full text. First, Anthropic explicitly exposes effort level as a controllable inference-budget axis and compares models using score versus cost per task across effort settings. That implies joint design of model behavior and inference policy: low effort for throughput and low cost, higher effort for longer deliberation or more steps. Second, Sonnet 5.5 is repeatedly described as using fewer output tokens, doing less unnecessary web search, asking fewer blocking questions, and batching tool calls more often. Those are usually signs of post-training that shapes agent policy rather than simple prompting: when to call tools, whether to parallelize them, when to stop gathering information, and how aggressively to compress the response. Third, the claim that it improves without changing prompts suggests the gain is not prompt-specific tuning alone; the model’s internal preferences and action priors have shifted. Fourth, the safety note says its cyber capability is now comparable to Opus 5, which triggers stronger safeguards and fallbacks. That indicates capability growth has crossed a risk threshold, and release policy is coupled to capability evaluation. From a foundation-model perspective, the interesting object is the full stack: pretraining, agentic post-training, effort-controlled inference, tool-use policy, and safety gating.b:T510,The most striking public number is 70.6% on Terminal-Bench 4.0 for Sonnet 5.5 versus 10.3% for Sonnet 5, a very large jump. The post also states that at Medium effort, Sonnet 5.5 exceeds Sonnet 5’s best score on Terminal-Bench for less than one-tenth of the cost per task. On FrontierCode, Sonnet 5.5 at High effort matches GPT-6 Sol’s best score at roughly one-fifth of the cost per task, and is 10 points above Sonnet 5 at the same effort while costing about one-fifteenth as much per task. On CursorBench, Sonnet 5.5 at Low effort beats Sonnet 5’s best score for under one-tenth of the cost, and its best score is within about two points of Opus 5.5. On AA-Briefcase, a long-horizon knowledge-work benchmark, Sonnet 5.5 at Medium effort beats Sonnet 5’s best score at about one-ninth of the cost. On GDPval-AA, it is only two points below Opus 5.5 and roughly 400 points above Sonnet 5. The real-world anecdotes are also informative: across 118 app builds, Sonnet 5.5 averaged 3.6 iterations per build versus 7.7 for Opus 5; in offline Slackbot evals, it used about 14% fewer output tokens without prompt changes; in Unity’s multi-step editor and coding benchmark, it completed 90% of tasks. The common pattern is not just better answers, but faster convergence to a usable solution.c:T4e4,Read it with attention to three things. First, focus on the score-versus-cost-per-task plots across effort levels rather than isolated benchmark numbers. The real question is how the Pareto frontier moves: whether low effort now covers capability regions that previously required much higher effort, and whether high effort still extends the ceiling. Second, pay close attention to the behavioral descriptions: batched tool calls, fewer steps, less web search, fewer output tokens, and fewer mid-build clarification questions. Those are external symptoms of changed training signals and are more informative than generic capability claims. Third, read the safety section carefully, especially why a Sonnet-tier model now needs cyber safeguards similar to stronger models. If the system card is available, prioritize the evaluation protocol, the definition of effort, the accounting of task cost, and the automated behavioral audit metrics. The most useful open questions are: how much of the cost reduction comes from token compression versus step compression; whether effort mainly increases reasoning tokens, tool calls, or search depth; and how much of the coding jump comes from stronger base-model knowledge versus more mature agent post-training.d:T70a,The paper analyzes discrete heavy-ball momentum dynamics in a river-valley model of the loss landscape. The geometry is decomposed into a low-loss, low-curvature, slowly bending river manifold and sharp orthogonal valley directions. The optimization challenge is not merely to descend, but to move quickly along the river without letting oscillations in the sharp directions destabilize the trajectory.

The main result is that momentum enlarges the largest tolerable learning rate by a leading factor of roughly (1+β)/(1-β) relative to vanilla GD. This factor is classical for quadratic objectives, but the nontrivial part here is showing that it still governs the leading stability threshold in a non-convex setting where the river and valley subspaces rotate and interact over time. Because of that rotation, gradient and momentum components leak across subspaces, so one cannot reuse a fixed-eigenspace argument.

To handle this, the authors develop a new inductive analysis that tracks both gradient and momentum projections onto river and valley directions, while controlling their mutual interference across iterations. A second key conclusion is interpretive: for a flat and slowly spinning river, momentum itself does not substantially change the intrinsic speed along the river. The acceleration mainly comes from enabling a much larger stable learning rate. In that sense, momentum acts more as a stabilizer that permits aggressive step sizes than as a direct source of extra river-direction acceleration.

The paper also improves the largest tolerable learning-rate bound for vanilla GD in the same river-valley framework. That side result suggests the proof machinery is not narrowly tailored to momentum alone, but strengthens the analytical understanding of the landscape model itself.e:T5fb,From the available full-text content, this is primarily a theory paper, with experiments serving to validate the mechanism rather than to establish a new large-scale training recipe. The empirical phenomenon tied to the theory is clear: under WSD-like schedules, a long high-LR phase can produce higher observed loss than cosine scheduling because of oscillations across sharp valley walls, while still making faster progress along the river. Once the learning rate is sharply decayed, those oscillations are suppressed and the hidden progress becomes visible as a lower final loss.

The paper also mentions synthetic river-valley loss constructions for visualization and connects its analysis to prior probing of LLM pretraining landscapes in Wen et al. (2025). The most important empirical checks are therefore: (1) momentum remains stable at larger learning rates on synthetic river-valley landscapes; (2) the predicted stability-threshold enlargement matches the observed stable region; and (3) the seemingly paradoxical pattern of higher intermediate loss but better final optimization can be explained by separating river progress from valley oscillation.

The provided text does not include full tables, model sizes, dataset sizes, or exact improvement numbers, so those should not be invented. When reading the original, the most useful experimental artifacts to extract are the stability-boundary plots, the comparisons against vanilla GD, and any quantitative alignment between the theorem and observed training dynamics.f:T58c,Read it in the order of phenomenon, model, theorem, then proof technique.

Start with the introduction and lock onto the practical question: why can a long high-LR phase plus sharp decay outperform smoother schedules, and what role does momentum play there? Then jump to Theorem 4.1. That is the central statement of the paper: the maximum stable learning rate is enlarged by about (1+β)/(1-β).

After that, spend time on Lemma 5.1 and the surrounding proof structure. This is where the real novelty sits. The important part is not only the final factor, but how the authors control dynamics when the river and valley subspaces rotate and when gradient and momentum components interfere with each other. While reading, focus on three things: how the river/valley projections are defined and updated, what assumptions encode “flat” and “slow-spinning,” and how the error terms are prevented from accumulating into instability.

If time is limited, read the theorem statements, proof sketches, and the main figures first, then return to the technical induction details. Also check the remark on the improved vanilla-GD learning-rate bound; it helps assess whether the analytical framework has broader value beyond the momentum case.

A careful read will likely take 1.5 to 2.5 hours. For readers focused on training stability and optimizer dynamics, this is worth reading with equations and figures side by side.10:T747,MoSAR, short for Mixture of Semantic Attention Regimes, inserts a lightweight router on the post-RoPE Q/K pathway. The router is bilinear and conditions on both queries and keys, rather than routing only one side. That choice is important: routing only queries gives row-wise structure, and routing only keys gives column-wise structure, but neither can localize useful two-dimensional regions of the attention matrix before dense similarities are computed. With both sides routed, the interaction between token i and token j selects a regime.

Each semantic regime defines a distance gate g(d). The gate is not just a simple exponential decay. It has a near-distance plateau where RoPE interactions are largely preserved, a transition region with smooth attenuation, and a low-relevance region where interactions become small enough to approximate away. This avoids the semantic discontinuity of hard local masks and makes approximability an outcome of the learned geometry.

The routing is input-conditioned and applied after positional encoding, so regime selection depends on contextual token representations that already carry positional structure. That is why the paper uses the term semantic regimes: locality is not determined by distance alone. MoSAR-Cost adds an explicit pressure toward shorter expected reach, biasing the router toward cheaper regimes and turning the quality-efficiency trade-off into a trainable objective.

Relative to prior efficient attention methods, the differences are clear. It does not predefine a sparse pattern; it predefines a set of decay laws and lets the data choose among them. It does not start by deleting edges; it first learns continuous attenuation and then exploits low-value regions for approximation. And it treats efficient attention as a geometry-learning problem rather than only an operator-design problem.11:T5f3,The main study uses 500M-parameter models trained from scratch and compares dense RoPE, fixed-locality baselines, MoSAR, and the cost-aware MoSAR-Cost. The headline result is that adaptive controlled decay outperforms both dense RoPE and fixed locality among the evaluated variants at 4096 and 8192 tokens, with MoSAR achieving the best perplexity in those long-context settings. The gains appear to be concentrated where long-range structure matters, which is aligned with the method’s goal.

The more interesting result is the inference-time approximation study. The authors replace soft mixture routing with deterministic top-1 routing at inference, effectively discretizing the learned geometry. This substantially reduces expected normalized reach while keeping perplexity competitive under length extrapolation. That suggests the learned regimes are not merely a training-time regularizer; they induce a geometry that can be compressed into a cheaper routing policy. MoSAR-Cost pushes the trade-off further toward lower reach, with some language-modeling cost, making the quality-versus-compute curve explicit rather than reporting a single operating point.

The most useful ablations are likely the ones on one-sided versus two-sided routing, number of regimes, the role of smooth transition regions, strength of cost regularization, and extrapolation behavior. The key question is not only whether perplexity improves, but whether perplexity, reach, and approximation error move in a stable and interpretable way.12:T412,Start with the introduction and lock onto the paper’s reframing: the target is not a better hand-designed sparse pattern, but a learned decay geometry over attention interactions. Then go to the method section and focus on three pieces: the definition of a semantic regime, the bilinear query-key router, and the MoSAR-Cost objective. It is worth understanding why routing is applied after RoPE rather than before it.

In the experiments, prioritize figures and tables that show perplexity across context lengths, expected or normalized reach, and the gap between soft mixture routing and deterministic top-1 routing. If time is limited, try to answer three questions: do the learned regimes actually separate into different locality behaviors, do those behaviors vary with layer and token semantics, and is the inference-time discretization loss small enough to matter in practice. Around 35 to 50 minutes should be enough for a solid skim. For replication judgment, the appendix on implementation details and extra ablations is necessary.13:T421,The analysis has three parts. First, it characterizes the population-optimal UDM predictor. Because uniform corruption mixes preserved tokens and random vocabulary tokens, every subset of observed positions defines a hypothesis about which context tokens are reliable. Time does not create new hypotheses; it reweights them through the noise level, with a signal-to-noise quantity such as rho_t = K alpha_t / (1 - alpha_t) controlling how much the observed context should be trusted. Second, the work studies the empirical finite-data oracle. In language data, the discrete sequence space is huge, and training examples are typically far apart. A corrupted sequence therefore remains closer to its original clean sequence than to competing examples over most noise levels, causing the posterior to concentrate and reducing the effect of time-dependent reweighting. Third, it derives a bound linking empirical time sensitivity to a post-corruption separation margin. The bound weakens near the high-noise endpoint, where multiple hypotheses become plausible.14:T4d5,LAVOIR builds on the Laya/Jev interface for typed decisions. The original input contains the question type, instruction, option markers, and the state. LAVOIR appends a slot block for candidate missing information. A single forward pass returns both the decision distribution and a predicted VOI score for each slot. The model keeps Laya’s ModernBERT-large decision head and adds a zero-initialized segment embedding so that, when no slots are present, the logits match Laya exactly in fp32. The VOI head reads each slot representation together with features of the current decision distribution and predicts a scalar value. It is a small MLP with about 0.27M parameters, LayerNorm, a 256-unit GELU hidden layer, and a scalar output. A Gini-based cap constrains the predicted value by the maximum expected gain of a calibrated posterior, which prevents the model from over-asking when it is already confident. Training labels are produced without human annotation: schema rules define the gold decision for complete profiles, an LLM verbalizes messages and answers, exact posteriors are computed by enumeration, and realized gains are measured as the increase in the model probability assigned to the gold decision after asking a slot.15:T41b,The method defines the original routed MoE output for a token as a consensus, meaning the weighted mixture to be preserved, not an assumption that experts agree. Each expert is represented through its residual relative to this consensus. For a fixed layer input, RAZOR derives an exact single-deletion identity for the local output change. The identity accounts for survivor weight renormalization and, when applicable, router-selected refill that promotes an unselected expert into the active set. Without refill, the deletion shift depends on the deviation between the removed expert output and the consensus, scaled by its routing weight; this already shows why gate mass and output norm are insufficient. With refill, the promoted expert’s residual can compensate for or amplify the removed contribution. RAZOR runs only forward passes on calibration tokens, aggregates local deletion damage per expert within each layer, and prunes the most replaceable experts under the target budget. It avoids gradients, subset search, and recovery training.16:T4dd,The paper uses a roofline-style analysis to separate weight traffic from KV-cache traffic during decode. For a model with P parameters and weight width wp, a batch-one decode step performs roughly 2P FLOPs and reads P·wp bytes, giving about 1 FLOP/B for BF16 weights. An H100 SXM has a BF16 dense ridge point around 295 FLOP/B, so this regime is bandwidth-bound by a large margin. KV attention has the same structure: one query attending over s cached tokens costs about 4sdh FLOPs per head and reads 2sdh·wkv bytes, again about 1 FLOP/B in BF16. GQA improves reuse by the query-to-KV head group factor g, but even g=8 gives only about 8 FLOP/B, still far below the ridge point. The paper then explains why batching helps weight reads but not KV reads. Weight bytes can be amortized across requests; KV bytes cannot, because each sequence owns its cache. The techniques are organized into five mechanism classes: quantization reduces element width, token eviction reduces retained tokens, paging manages KV blocks and fragmentation, prefix caching shares common prompt prefixes, and memory tiering moves KV state across HBM, host DRAM, and storage. The paper’s comparison discipline is to keep analytical claims and measured claims separate.17:T422,The most important quantitative examples are concrete. For Llama-3-70B in BF16, the weight footprint is about 140 GB, already larger than a single 80 GB HBM device. The KV cache size is Bkv = 2·L·nkv·dh·wkv·s. With L=80, nkv=8, dh=128, and BF16 storage, Llama-3-70B uses 327,680 KV bytes per token. A single 128k-token sequence therefore adds about 42 GB of KV state; a 1M-token sequence requires about 328 GB, more than the aggregate HBM of four 80 GB H100 devices. The arithmetic-intensity numbers are equally important: BF16 batch-one decode sits near 1 FLOP/B for weight traffic, while the H100 ridge point is about 295 FLOP/B. On the attention side, GQA with group factor 8 still reaches only about 8 FLOP/B. Weight-side batching approaches the ridge only at a batch size around 295 in this simplified model, while KV-side intensity is invariant to batch size. The paper’s measurement contribution is to make these comparisons explicit rather than mixing throughput, latency, memory footprint, and quality degradation under incompatible settings.18:T45c,The analysis separates two mechanisms. Feedback exposure describes where quantization error is structurally dangerous. In models such as Huginn-3.5B and Recurrent-Llama, recurrence passes through a non-residual loop-entry adapter. Quantizing this adapter perturbs the recurrent state without an identity path, so the error is injected into future steps. Quantizing the residual core is much less harmful because the identity path buffers the perturbation. The paper uses the local spectral radius of the per-step Jacobian as a stability diagnostic for possible amplification directions. Calibration blindness is a different issue. GPTQ builds a Hessian proxy H=X^T X from calibration activations. If X is collected only from the first invocation of a shared layer, it can miss directions that appear in later loop steps. The paper compares this one-step calibration with trajectory calibration, which accumulates activations across all recurrent invocations. It also distinguishes per-channel INT4 from grouped INT4 with group size 128, where the latter reduces quantization error at the cost of extra scale storage.19:T4c7,The Huginn-3.5B diagnostic is the clearest result. On 100 GSM8K examples, bf16 reaches 34% exact match, while per-channel INT4 on both the loop-entry adapter and recurrent core drops to 0%. A 50-prompt token-agreement diagnostic localizes the damage: adapter-only INT4 gives 10.5% agreement, adapter plus core gives 10.6%, and core-only INT4 keeps 87.9%. This points to the non-residual adapter as the main failure point. COCONUT behaves differently because it reuses a residual GPT-2 stack; agreement increases from 44.8% after the base forward to 61.5% after eight latent recurrences. Standard one-step sensitivity metrics fail to predict the Huginn result: small random input perturbations rank the adapter as the least sensitive among 17 projections, and Hessian trace highlights a core projection even though quantizing the adapter is far more damaging. Grouped INT4 avoids the immediate collapse, but one-step GPTQ still underperforms round-to-nearest on five of nine checkpoints. More calibration prompts do not remove the mismatch, because the missing information is the set of directions visited later in the trajectory. Linear-filter and Mamba controls show that feedback exposure is not specific to Transformers.1a:T85b,The method has three main components. First, it redefines the reusable object. Instead of caching whole requests or token prefixes, DynBranch stores a completed branch subgraph together with the exact input that triggered it and the external reads it depended on. A later request may consume the entry only if the input matches exactly and those reads are still fresh; any write to the recorded dependencies invalidates the entry. This is important for agent workflows where retrieval and tool outputs depend on mutable external state, so prompt matching alone is insufficient.

Second, it makes unresolved branches addressable. The mechanism relies on a stable coordinate provided by the workflow template: the identity of a decision point known before the branch resolves, such as a router stage or a fixed tool-selection site. Using this coordinate, the system can launch candidate branch subgraphs as fills during the resolution window. A fill is speculative execution of a candidate downstream subgraph before the actual branch is chosen. The safety rule is strict: fills remain invisible until branch selection occurs and a canonical demand authorizes their use via exact match and freshness checks. Wrong predictions therefore waste resources but do not alter semantics.

Third, DynBranch uses a two-level admission controller. One level decides whether to generate candidates at all; the second decides whether each candidate fill should run. The decision criterion is whether expected benefit exceeds a load-dependent price. That price is not a fixed threshold. It reads current decode occupancy and KV occupancy from the engine and prices work over the fill's execution horizon. This allows aggressive speculation at low load and automatic contraction at high load. The paper also indicates that the controller learns benefit estimates from delayed feedback rather than relying only on static heuristics.

Implementation-wise, DynBranch sits at the model-API boundary between agent harnesses and unmodified SGLang or vLLM backends. That makes it a serving middleware design rather than a new agent runtime or a model-side method.1b:T737,The main setup uses Qwen3-32B on 4×H200, with additional results on Qwen3-8B with an RTX 4090. The paper first motivates the problem empirically: in measured coding, research, and scientific agents, 45%–57% of end-to-end latency is downstream time serialized after model output. That establishes that the branch-resolution barrier is not a corner case.

On performance, DynBranch reduces mean latency by up to 32% relative to the strongest prior system for each workload on the same GPUs, and by 46%–66% relative to a no-reuse floor. When reuse is disabled and only early fill is kept, mean latency still drops by 8.5%–34.4% on model-driven paths, and user-turn latency drops by 46.6%–47.7% in human-interaction settings. This is an important result: the gains do not come only from cross-request reuse; overlapping work during the branch-resolution window already matters.

The experimental structure also suggests three points worth checking closely. First is correctness: the paper states that workflow results are preserved, consistent with the design where speculative fills become visible only after canonical authorization. Second is load sensitivity: the two-level controller should show different admission behavior under different occupancy regimes, otherwise the claim of safe speculation under load would be weak. Third is generality: the authors report that benefits persist across backbone families, which suggests the effect comes from workflow structure and serving dynamics rather than a model-specific artifact.

The provided text does not include the full result tables, so it is not safe to invent more workload-level absolute latency or hit-rate numbers. But the central empirical message is clear: the combination of reuse, early fill, and load-aware admission outperforms caching-only or speculation-only baselines.1c:T525,The framework maximizes draft-token acceptance subject to an f-divergence constraint between the mentored output distribution and the target distribution. The total-variation case is used as a warm-up and has a clean geometric structure: the set of optimal solutions is the intersection of a coordinate-wise hyperrectangle induced by the drafter and target, the probability simplex, and the active divergence boundary. This set is large enough to contain the optimal solutions for all strictly convex f-divergences. For a general strictly convex f-divergence, the optimal mentored distribution is unique and has a coordinate-wise clamping form, π* = max{αq, min{p, βq}}, where q is the target distribution, p is the drafter distribution, and 0 ≤ α < 1 < β. Intuitively, each token probability is kept within lower and upper multiplicative bands around the target probability, while moving along a one-dimensional path from the target toward the drafter. A notable property is that this path does not depend on the chosen f-divergence. The paper further connects the composite output distribution to boosting by developing a multiclass extension of self-normalized boosting and by showing how the drafter-target mixture can satisfy boosting-style conditions when the two models have useful edge complementarity.1d:T4fb,The method, PISA, builds a coarse-to-fine pyramid over key blocks. The finest level contains fixed-size blocks, and each coarser level is formed by pooling adjacent lower-level representations. Instead of scoring every fine-grained block, routing starts at the coarsest level. At each level, the query computes LogSumExp scores over only a bounded candidate set, keeps the Top-K candidates, and expands those selected blocks into their children at the next finer level. This repeats until the leaf blocks are reached.

With a fixed branching factor, Top-K budget, and leaf block size, each query evaluates only a bounded number of candidates per level, and the number of levels is O(log N). That gives O(log N) routing per query and O(N log N) routing over the sequence. The final sparse attention is then computed over the selected original blocks.

The practical angle is important here. The authors implement hardware-aware Triton kernels that fuse multi-level routing and LSE scoring, so intermediate candidates do not need to be repeatedly materialized and moved through memory. They also avoid constructing a dense query-key score matrix. That matters because many sparse-attention ideas fail to deliver real speedups once kernel and memory traffic are taken seriously.1e:T91f,方法核心是 acoustic-to-text KV compression。基础模型是 MiniCPM-o 4.5，它按 1 秒为单位处理 duplex unit，每个 unit 内包含音频 token、一个 native 的 <|listen|> 或 <|speak|> 决策，以及后续可能的响应生成。作者在每个 unit 的第一个 native decision 之后，插入一个可选的 transcription segment，用新引入的 <|asr_start|> 和 <|asr_end|> 包围。这个 segment 只作为语言模型上下文中的文本记忆存在，不送入 speech decoder，也不会被说出来。

推理时，模型自己决定是否打开 side channel。如果预测出 <|asr_start|>，就贪心解码最多 64 个文本 token，直到 <|asr_end|>；若超长或遇到控制 token，则强制闭合。随后，当 resident KV cache 超过目标预算时，系统驱逐更早的 acoustic states，但保留这些 transcript token，并保留一个最近的 acoustic window 以覆盖转写延迟。于是，较老的语音内容以文本形式留在上下文里，最近一段仍保留原始声学细节，形成“远处文本化、近处声学化”的混合记忆。

训练上，作者没有单独接一个 ASR 模型，而是复用原有 LM backbone，只用 LoRA 适配 side channel。监督信号来自带词级强制对齐的转写数据：每个词根据其声学结束时间和额外延迟 δ 被分配到某个 1 秒 unit 中，每个词只出现在一个目标 transcription segment。仅用 transcription cross-entropy 会改变原模型的原生对话行为，所以作者再加入 token-level knowledge distillation。具体做法是：冻结原始 full-duplex 模型作为 teacher，让它在同一音频上、但不插入 transcription segment 地运行；收集 teacher 在所有 native prediction positions 的全词表分布；student 在插入 transcription segment 的序列上训练时，对这些 native 位置同时拟合 teacher 分布。这样 side channel 学会转写，同时尽量不扰动原有 listen/speak 决策、停顿处理、打断响应等行为。

这个设计和“单独训练一个流式 ASR，再把文本喂回模型”相比，有两个差异。其一，推理时不需要额外 ASR 模块，部署更简单；其二，蒸馏约束的是 full-duplex 原生行为，而不只是转写准确率，因此目标更贴近语音对话模型的系统需求。1f:T828,实验围绕三件事展开：KV 峰值控制、长时语音理解、实时性与交互行为保持。主模型是 MiniCPM-o 4.5。作者指出，转写后的文本记忆大约每秒只占 3 个序列位置，而原始 acoustic history 约占 17 个位置，这给压缩上限提供了直观背景。

在十分钟 LongSpeech session 上，与“同样经过 side-channel 适配但不做 eviction”的模型相比，峰值 streaming KV cache 大小下降 64.6%。这说明收益主要来自“文本化后驱逐旧声学 KV”，而不是仅仅插入 transcript token。长时理解方面，论文称该方法在 transcription、temporal question answering、summarization 上都优于 native streaming，说明文本记忆不仅节省显存，也改善了对长程语言内容的保持。

实时性方面，在 4K KV cache budget 下，包含 transcription 与 cache eviction 在内的整套处理，在 H100、batch size 1 上对每个 1 秒音频单元平均耗时 461 ms；在 10,039 个 unit 的测量中没有 deadline miss。这一点很关键，因为方法成立的前提就是 side channel 能在 listening-time slack 内完成。若超过 1 秒窗口，就会破坏 full-duplex 实时性。

行为保持方面，Full-Duplex-Bench 上 pause-handling、turn-taking、interruption 等指标与原模型相当，支持了蒸馏约束确实在抑制 side-channel 训练对原生交互行为的扰动。摘要还提到 WER 统计会把 skipped/truncated segments 计入错误，这意味着作者没有通过只评估成功打开 side channel 的片段来美化结果。

从方法设计推断，值得关注的消融应包括：只做 CE 转写监督、不做 KD 时 native behavior 的退化；不同 KV budget 下理解与实时性的折中；recent acoustic window 大小对性能的影响；以及 emission delay δ 对转写完整性和驱逐时机的影响。给出的全文片段没有完整表格数字，但主结论已经足够明确：在线文本化旧语音上下文，可以在不破坏 full-duplex 交互的前提下显著降低峰值 KV，并提升长时理解。20:T95a,The core method is acoustic-to-text KV compression. The base model, MiniCPM-o 4.5, processes interaction in 1-second duplex units. Each unit contains audio tokens, a native <|listen|> or <|speak|> decision, and possibly response generation. After the first native decision in each unit, the authors insert an optional transcription segment delimited by two new tokens, <|asr_start|> and <|asr_end|>. This segment stays in the language-model context as textual memory, but it is excluded from the speech decoder input and from spoken output.

At inference time, the model decides whether to open the side channel. If it predicts <|asr_start|>, it greedily decodes up to 64 text tokens until <|asr_end|>; if the segment overruns or hits a control token, the segment is forcibly closed. When the resident KV cache exceeds a target budget, older acoustic states are evicted while transcript tokens are retained, together with a recent acoustic window that covers transcription delay. The resulting memory is hybrid: older context is preserved in text form, while recent context remains acoustically grounded.

Training avoids a separate ASR model. The side channel is adapted with LoRA on top of the existing LM backbone. Supervision comes from transcribed utterances with word-level forced alignment. Each word is assigned to a 1-second unit based on its acoustic endpoint plus an additional emission delay delta, and each word appears in exactly one target transcription segment. However, pure transcription cross-entropy would alter the model's native conversational behavior. To prevent that, the paper adds token-level knowledge distillation. A frozen teacher, namely the original full-duplex model without transcription segments, processes the same audio. Its full-vocabulary distributions at native prediction positions are collected, and the student is trained to match them at those positions while also learning the inserted transcription segments. This constrains changes to native listen/speak decisions, pause handling, interruption behavior, and response generation.

Compared with a pipeline that runs a separate streaming ASR and feeds text back into the model, this design has two practical differences. It removes the need for an extra ASR module at inference, and it regularizes the adapted model toward preserving full-duplex behavior rather than optimizing transcription alone.21:T989,The experiments focus on three aspects: peak KV control, long-form speech understanding, and real-time behavioral preservation. The base model is MiniCPM-o 4.5. The paper notes that transcript memory uses roughly 3 sequence positions per second of speech, versus about 17 for acoustic history, which gives a clear intuition for why the compression can matter.

On 10-minute LongSpeech sessions, peak streaming KV cache size is reduced by 64.6% relative to the same side-channel-adapted model without eviction. This is an important comparison because it isolates the gain from converting old acoustic context into text and then evicting acoustic KV, rather than from simply inserting transcript tokens. On long-form understanding, the method improves transcription, temporal question answering, and summarization over native streaming, suggesting that textual memory is not only cheaper but also more effective for retaining long-range linguistic content.

On real-time feasibility, under a 4K KV-cache budget the full processing path, including transcription and cache eviction, averages 461 ms per 1-second audio unit on an H100 at batch size 1. Across 10,039 measured units, there are no deadline misses. This matters because the whole method depends on finishing side-channel work within the listening-time slack.

On behavioral preservation, results on Full-Duplex-Bench show comparable pause handling, turn taking, and interruption performance. That supports the claim that token-level distillation successfully constrains the side-channel adaptation. The paper also states that skipped or truncated transcription segments are counted as errors in WER, which is a useful detail because it avoids reporting only successful side-channel openings.

The most informative ablations are likely the following: cross-entropy-only transcription training versus CE plus KD, to show how native behavior degrades without distillation; different KV budgets to expose the trade-off between memory and understanding; the size of the retained recent acoustic window; and the effect of the emission delay delta on transcription completeness and eviction timing. The provided text does not include all tables, so it would be unsafe to add more exact numbers, but the main result is already clear: online textualization of old speech context can substantially reduce peak KV while preserving full-duplex interaction and improving long-range understanding.22:T57d,This is a skim-to-light-read paper, especially if you care about speech LMs, unified memory, or streaming multimodal inference. Start with the Introduction to set the boundary correctly: the goal is not better ASR, but online memory compression inside a full-duplex speech LM. Then read the method section carefully, focusing on where the transcription segment is inserted, how the side channel is opened during inference, and how eviction is triggered. Those details determine whether the approach is actually deployable without changing the backbone architecture.

The training section is worth extra attention. In particular, look at how forced alignment assigns words to units and how token-level KD is applied only at native prediction positions. That is the most technically interesting part, because it explains why LoRA adaptation can add transcription ability without noticeably damaging native listening and speaking behavior.

In the experiments, prioritize the tables on peak KV usage, LongSpeech understanding tasks, and Full-Duplex-Bench behavior preservation. If there is a latency timeline or a budget sweep, read that too, since it directly shows engineering feasibility. Around 30–40 minutes should be enough for the key ideas. If you only want to judge transferability to another speech LM, read the method figure, the training objective, and the real-time result under the 4K budget.23:T7da,The work has three technical layers. The first is dataset construction. The authors build a benchmark of 317 manually verified knowledge reversals across medicine, law, software, and platform policy. Each case is grounded in dated official sources and includes a clear validity boundary between what used to be correct and what is correct now. This is more precise than generic knowledge-conflict datasets because the temporal transition is explicit.

The second layer is behavioral evaluation. The paper measures poisoning in a strict way: only cases where the same model is correct without retrieval but becomes wrong after seeing outdated evidence count as stale-document poisoning. That isolates the net harm introduced by retrieval. The experiments compare neutral retrieval prompts with prompts that explicitly instruct the model to follow the document, separating natural deference from instruction-amplified deference.

The third layer is temporal-applicability and internal-mechanism analysis. On 50 source-verified reversals, the authors hold the historical evidence text fixed and vary only the evaluation date. This tests whether the model changes its trust in the same evidence when it moves from valid to superseded. Models react only modestly to date changes alone, but respond much more strongly when the validity boundary is made explicit. That means the problem is not simply missing date tokens; it is a failure to map dates into applicability judgments. Causal interventions further show that information about when evidence applies can directly change the final answer, while matched control interventions do little. Attention analysis suggests that some components contribute early to this evidence-arbitration process.

The paper also evaluates a fixed hybrid reranker that combines semantic relevance, recency, and supersession cues. It reduces poisoning when metadata are reliable, showing that part of the problem can be mitigated on the retrieval side, though not fully solved there.24:T643,The evaluation covers 12 models: six API models and six open-weight models ranging from 4B to 72B parameters. Cross-domain behavioral experiments focus on Qwen2.5 and Llama-3.1 at both small and large scales. Temporal-applicability experiments include Qwen2.5-7B/72B and Llama-3.1-8B/70B. Causal analyses focus on the 72B and 70B models.

Three result groups matter most. First, outdated retrieval flips otherwise correct answers even without explicit document-following instructions: 30% for Llama and 37% for Qwen. So the failure is not just a prompt-engineering artifact; the models already over-defer to retrieved evidence. Second, explicit instructions to follow the document raise those flip rates to 66% and 75%, showing that instruction following amplifies the selective-trust failure. Third, matched up-to-date evidence is followed almost universally, which means the models are not generally bad at using retrieval. They are bad at deciding when retrieved evidence should no longer be trusted.

The paper also finds that recent medical reversals are harder than long-established ones, suggesting that near-boundary temporal drift is especially difficult. In the fixed-evidence, changed-date setup, changing the evaluation date alone produces only limited behavioral adjustment, whereas making the validity boundary explicit causes much larger answer changes. That contrast is central because it separates date awareness from applicability reasoning. The reranker experiments show consistent reductions in poisoning when recency and supersession cues are available and metadata are trustworthy.25:T415,This is a skim paper, but it deserves a careful skim if you work on RAG, continual pretraining, or knowledge updating. Start with the introduction and task definition to see how stale-document poisoning is separated from generic retrieval errors, adversarial context, and parametric-versus-context conflict. Then read the benchmark construction section closely enough to understand the source criteria, how validity boundaries are defined, and how the authors verify that the old answer was genuinely correct at an earlier time.

In the experiments, prioritize three parts: the main poisoning-rate table, especially the gap between neutral prompts and follow-the-document prompts; the fixed-evidence, changed-date temporal-applicability setup; and the causal intervention plus reranker sections.
